But I'll come inside, take off my shoes, wash my hands, and I'll go to the stove to fix myself a plate of something warm. Usually some rice and beans, or maybe a nice chicken stew. I make a very hearty chicken stew, doctor, the way my mother taught me, with the thick broth and the little pearl onions. \n\nSo I'll dish out a normal plate, just a regular portion for a lady my age, and I'll sit down at the kitchen table. But then... well, I finish that plate, and that heavy quiet I was telling you about is still just sitting there in the room with me. So I'll get up and go back to the stove for a little more. And then I just... I just keep going back. I'll stand right there at the counter with the serving spoon, getting another scoop, and then another. \n\nAnd by the time I finally stop and look up at the clock above the sink... maybe only an hour has passed. An hour, or maybe an hour and a half at most. It's so terribly heavy. I think \"ashamed\" is really the best word for it. Afterward, when the pot is empty and I put the spoon down, and that awful quiet rushes right back into the kitchen... I just feel so deeply ashamed and sad about what I've done. \n\nBecause, you see, cooking and food... they used to mean something entirely different to me. We'd all sit around the table together, and it was a blessing, you know? It was how I showed my family that I loved them, how I kept them strong and healthy. \n\nBut now... now I look at what I'm doing, standing there alone at the counter, just shoveling it in without even tasting it, and I realize I've taken this beautiful thing that used to mean family and caring, and I've turned it into this... this ugly little secret. \n\nAnd that's exactly what it is, a secret that I feel like I have to hide from everyone. Especially the people at church. I've been going to that same parish for forty years, doctor. I'm on the hospitality committee, I bake the casseroles for the potlucks and the funeral luncheons, I organize the bake sales. The ladies there, they all know me as someone who cooks to serve the Lord and serve the community. If Martha or Susan or any of those ladies knew what I was doing in my own kitchen on a Tuesday afternoon... if they knew I was just gorging myself on food meant for three people... oh, I think I would just die of embarrassment. I really would. \n\nSo I wash the pot as fast as I can, and I scrub the bowl, and I put everything away so there's no evidence left. My mother, God rest her soul, she was very strict about table manners when my sisters and I were growing up. She'd sit right there at the head of the table and watch us to make sure we weren't rushing. So because of that, I've always been the last one to finish my meal. Even when my husband was alive—he was a fast eater, he'd be completely done with his roast beef and potatoes, and I'd still be cutting up my first slice of meat, just taking my time and savoring it. \n\nBut lately... when this happens, when that awful quiet in the house pushes me into the kitchen... it's like I'm a completely different person. I eat so incredibly fast. I don't even sit down at the table like a civilized person, I just stand right there by the counter. I've always taken such pride in savoring a good, home-cooked meal, and now I'm just standing there shoveling it in like someone is going to snatch the bowl away from me if I don't finish it that very second. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, doctor, that's exactly it. That is the whole reason I finally decided I needed to come in and talk to somebody about this. Because... well, it's just so frightening when it happens. 

I'll be standing there, like I was telling you, right by the stove with the serving spoon in my hand, eating those warm comfort foods that just remind me of better days. And in my head, I am completely aware of what I'm doing. I will literally say to myself, sometimes even out loud to the empty room, I'll say, "Maria, that is enough. You are full, put the spoon down." I scold myself just like my mother used to scold me when I'd try to sneak a second piece of bread before supper. 

But... it's the strangest thing. Even while I'm telling myself to stop, my hand just... it still goes right back to the pot. It's like my arm doesn't even belong to me anymore. I just can't make myself stop. No matter how much I tell myself to quit, the hand just dips back into the stew or the beans and brings up another spoonful. It's a terrible feeling, doctor, to be standing in your own kitchen, in the house you've lived in for fifty years, and feel like you have absolutely no say over what your own body is doing. It's exactly what brings me in today, because once I start eating, I just cannot make myself stop, no matter how hard I try to talk some sense into myself.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit hard to explain, really. You see, I'm home most of the day now, have been for years and years, and the house gets so quiet. The days can feel awfully long when it's just you. So I cook. I make these big pots of things, you know, macaroni, thick stews, potatoes—real comfort foods that remind me of when the house was full of people and noise. \n\nBut what brings me in today is... well, it's what happens after I make them. Once I start eating those comfort foods, something just comes over me. I'll have a bowl, and I'll sit in my armchair to watch my programs, and I know I'm done. I tell myself, I even say it right out loud sometimes, I say, \"Maria, that is enough.\" I know I don't need another bite. But then... it's like my brain and my body aren't talking to each other anymore. \n\nBefore I even really decide to get up, my hand just goes right back to the pot on the stove. I'll scoop out more, and I just keep eating, like I can’t make myself stop. I'll be sitting there, telling myself the whole time that I shouldn't be doing this, that I've had plenty, but my hand just keeps going back for another spoonful, and then another. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing, because I've always been a sensible woman, but with this... I just can't seem to make myself put the spoon down.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you every time you cook and eat these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not every single time I cook, thank goodness. If it were every time, I don't know what I'd do with myself. But... well, it's been happening a lot more lately. You see, my dear friend Betty passed away a few months ago. We used to do everything together. We'd go to choir practice on Tuesdays, and then we had our little community garden plot we'd tend to on Thursdays. We'd grow the most wonderful tomatoes, doctor, big beefsteak ones. \n\nSince she's been gone, I still go to choir, and I still go down to the garden, but coming home... coming home is the hardest part. The house is just so empty. So I'll come back from singing, or from pulling weeds, and it's usually those quiet afternoons, maybe once or twice a week, that it really hits me. I'll make a pot of something warm, thinking it'll make me feel better, make the house smell like it used to. And that's when it happens. Usually once or twice a week, just sitting there in the quiet after I've been out doing the things Betty and I used to do together. It's like the silence is so loud I just have to fill it up, and before I know it, I'm back at the stove, scooping out another bowl, even though I'm not hungry in the slightest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor... guilty doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... well, it's this awful, heavy feeling that just settles right in my chest. You see, cooking used to be such a joyous thing for me. When my husband was alive, God rest his soul, and the kids were still running around the house, making a big pot of stew or a casserole... it was how I showed them I loved them. It meant family. It meant caring for the people I cherished most. I'd stand at that stove for hours, chopping and stirring, and it was a labor of love, it really was. \n\nBut now... now when I do this, when I eat and eat until I'm just stuffed and miserable... afterward, I just sit there in my chair, looking at the empty bowls, and I feel so deeply ashamed. It's like a dark cloud just comes right down over me. I feel so sad, doctor, just incredibly sad. Because I look at what I've done, and I realize I've taken something beautiful—something that used to bring people together around my table—and I've turned it into this... this secret, shameful thing. \n\nI'll be washing the dishes afterward, scrubbing the pot, and I just cry sometimes. I think to myself, \"What if the ladies at church knew?\" What if Pastor Thomas or Mary from the altar guild found out that I sit alone in my house and just gorge myself on macaroni until I can barely breathe? I'd die of embarrassment, I really would. I have to hide it from everyone. I make sure to take the trash out right away so no one sees the empty food containers, and if someone drops by, I rush to put everything away. It's exhausting, carrying around this secret, and the shame of it... it just eats away at me, it really does. It's a terrible thing to feel like you have to hide yourself from the people you pray with on Sundays.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, that's just it, isn't it? That's exactly the crux of the whole terrible matter. When you ask if I'm in control... oh, heavens, no. Not at all. And that's what's so frightening about it, really. \n\nLike I was trying to explain earlier, when I sit down with that first bowl of macaroni or stew—and I make a very good stew, doctor, with tender carrots and those little pearl onions, just the way my mother taught me—I'll be sitting there in my armchair, the one by the window where the light is best in the afternoon. And I'll finish that bowl, and I'll say it right out loud, just as clear as day. I'll say, \"Maria, that is enough. You are full, you are satisfied, and you do not need another bite.\" \n\nI know it in my head. I feel it in my stomach. But then... it's like a switch flips, or like someone else is driving the car, if you know what I mean. My hand just picks up that spoon, or the bowl, and before I can even process what's happening, I'm walking right back into the kitchen. I'll stand over the stove, and I'll just scoop more out. And the whole time—this is the part that makes me feel like I'm losing my mind—the whole time I'm doing it, I'm telling myself to stop. I'm thinking, \"Why are you doing this? Put the spoon down, Maria.\" But my hand just keeps moving. It goes from the pot to my mouth, over and over, like I'm a machine or something. I can't make myself stop, no matter how hard I try or how much I scold myself. It's like my own body just isn't listening to me anymore. I'm just along for the ride, and it's a dreadful, dreadful feeling to have no say in what your own two hands are doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these episodes of eating without control for more than 3 months?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that for a moment, doctor. Let me just look at the calendar in my head. You see, Betty passed away right at the tail end of the summer. I remember it so clearly because our community garden was just starting to turn, you know? It's that time of year when the nights get a little bit cooler and the tomatoes get those little splits in their skins. We had just picked the last of the really good, big beefsteak ones before she went into the hospital. And then the funeral... oh, it was a lovely service, it really was. Pastor Thomas spoke so beautifully about her generous spirit, and the choir sang her favorite hymns. \n\nBut anyway, after the funeral, for the first week or two, the ladies from the church were bringing by so many casseroles and baked zitis and things that I didn't even have to turn on my oven. It was only after all that stopped, when folks went back to their normal lives and I was left all alone in that quiet house, that I had to start cooking for myself again. And that is exactly when this whole dreadful business began. So, if I count it back... yes, it has been right about three months now. \n\nIt's been going on for about three months, though I tell you, some days it feels like it's been a lifetime. Every week just stretches out so long without her. I keep hoping, you know, I keep thinking to myself that maybe when spring comes around and I need to get the soil ready for planting again, maybe I'll finally snap out of this terrible habit. But yes, for these past three months, ever since my dear Betty left this earth, it's been exactly like I told you. Three solid months of sitting in that chair, getting up, eating when I know perfectly well I shouldn't, and just feeling so terribly ashamed of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen to you regularly, like on a weekly basis?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, they do. It's... it's almost every week, to be perfectly honest with you. Ever since my dear friend Betty passed, like I was telling you, this whole awful thing has been happening most weeks. Usually, I'd say it's about once or twice a week. \n\nAnd it's always, always in those terribly quiet afternoons. You see, I'll come home from choir practice—I still sing alto, have for near forty years now, though my voice isn't quite as strong as it was when my husband was alive—or I'll come inside from working out in the dirt, doing my gardening. I still try to keep up with the weeding, even though my knees just ache terribly if I kneel for too long. But I'll come inside, wash the soil off my hands at the kitchen sink, and the house is just... oh, doctor, it's just so still. It's a heavy, lonely kind of quiet. \n\nBetty and I used to sit out on the porch with a glass of iced tea after we pulled the weeds, just talking about the Sunday sermon or how the hydrangeas were doing. Now, there's just nobody. Just me and the ticking clock in the hallway. And that's exactly when it gets me. Once or twice a week, in those long, empty afternoons after choir or gardening, I'll start making a pot of macaroni or stew just to have something to do, to make the house smell like a home again, I suppose. And then before I know it, I'm eating and eating just to fill up all that quiet. So yes, it's a regular weekly thing now, Heaven help me, and I just don't know how to break out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're eating like this and can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Anxious... well, let me just ponder on that a second, doctor. Stressed. You know, I don't think I would use those particular words. \n\nWhen you say \"stressed,\" I think about back when my husband was alive and he got laid off from the mill back in the seventies. Now that was stress. I'd be up all night, pacing the floorboards, worrying over how we were going to afford the children's school shoes or keep the heat on during those terrible blizzards we used to get. My heart would just hammer in my chest, and I'd be running around all day trying to make a dollar stretch into five. \n\nBut this... what happens now with the eating... it isn't like that at all. It's almost the exact opposite, if that makes any sense. When I'm standing there at the stove, scooping out that macaroni or the stew, I'm not jittery or worked up. It's more like... oh, how do I explain it? It's like a thick fog just rolls right into my kitchen and wraps around my head. I don't feel nervous. I just feel sort of blank, or numb, just trying to fill up that awful, heavy silence I was telling you about. It's just me and the spoon and the quiet. \n\nNow, afterward... well, afterward I feel just sick with myself, and terribly ashamed, like I said before. I'll look at the empty pot and think, \"Maria, what on earth have you done?\" I'll just want to cry looking at the mess. But during the actual eating? No, I wouldn't call it anxiety or stress. I'm not panicked while my hand is moving. It's just a deep, deep loneliness, I suppose, and a sort of going through the motions to keep the quiet away. But no, not stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens because you're trying to cope with your feelings of loneliness and sadness after Betty passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you put it that way, doctor... I suppose... yes. Yes, I think you might have hit the nail right on the head there. \n\nYou know, it's funny how you can be doing something, just going through the motions day after day, and not really see the forest for the trees until someone else points it out to you. But when you say it out loud like that... coping with the loneliness... it makes a terrible amount of sense. \n\nBecause before Betty got sick, I never did this. Never in all my seventy-eight years. My husband, God rest his soul, he used to say I ate like a bird. \"Maria,\" he'd say, \"you're going to blow away in a stiff breeze if you don't eat more of that roast.\" I always just had a small plate, always stopped when I was full. Even when he passed, twenty years ago now, I didn't turn to the pantry. I turned to my church, and to my garden, and to my friends. To Betty. \n\nShe was the one who came over and sat with me every single afternoon that first year after he died. We'd just sit and shell peas on the porch, or fold laundry, not even talking sometimes, just... being there. Having another breathing soul in the house. \n\nAnd now she's gone, too. And my children, well, they're all grown and moved away—my eldest is out in California, and my youngest is up in Chicago, they have their own lives and their own families, and I can't expect them to be calling their old mother every day. So it's just me. And when I come into this house, and it's so quiet you can hear the refrigerator humming from two rooms away... I just feel this great big hole right in the center of my chest. It physically aches, doctor. It really does. \n\nAnd I think... I think when I start cooking, and then when I start eating all that macaroni or that stew... for just those few minutes, my mouth is busy, and my stomach is getting full, and it distracts me from how empty the rest of the house is. It's like I'm trying to fill up that hole in my chest with food, because I don't have Betty to fill it up with conversation anymore. So yes. Yes, I think you're exactly right. It's the sadness. It's just... it's just being so terribly, terribly sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the sadness you feel after Betty passed away directly triggers these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Directly triggers... well, now, I just don't know if I can say for sure how that part works, doctor. That word, 'trigger'... it sounds so mechanical, doesn't it? Like flipping a switch on the wall to turn on the porch light. You flip it up, the light comes on. But my mind, well, at my age, I don't think it works quite like a simple light switch. \n\nI mean, I know I just said a moment ago that the sadness is why I'm doing it. And I do believe that's the truth of it, I really do. But as for it being a... a direct trigger? I couldn't rightly say if it's that straightforward. Because sometimes the sadness is just there all day long, sitting on my chest heavy as a wet wool blanket, from the minute I wake up and see that the sun is shining and realize Betty isn't here to enjoy it. I'll look out the kitchen window at the hydrangeas—they're blooming so beautifully this year, big blue heads on them, she would have just loved them—and I'll feel that terrible ache. But I don't go eating right then in the morning. \n\nIt's only later. So is the sadness triggering it, or is it the quiet? Or is it both of them getting together in the afternoon and just wearing me down to the bone? I just... I couldn't tell you the exact science of it. I'll just be looking at Betty's old gardening gloves—I kept them, you know, they're still sitting on the porch rail right where she left them the Tuesday before she went into the hospital—and I'll feel so completely lost. And then the next thing I know, I'm standing at the stove making that macaroni. \n\nSo I don't know if the sadness directly fires it off like a starting pistol, or if I'm just wandering around my empty house trying to find something, anything at all, to do with my hands when the grief gets too loud. I just know my heart is broken, doctor, and the eating is what happens when I can't bear the quiet anymore. I'm sorry if that doesn't exactly answer your question, I just don't know the proper medical way to explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Within an hour or two... well, yes, I suppose it all happens rather quickly once the ball gets rolling. \n\nYou see, some afternoons... usually after I get home from choir practice over at the church, or maybe after I've been out working in the garden. The weeds have been just terrible this year, especially around my tomatoes, and my knees aren't what they used to be, so I get quite tired out by the time I come back indoors. But I'll come inside, take off my shoes, wash my hands, and I'll go to the stove to fix myself a plate of something warm. Usually some rice and beans, or maybe a nice chicken stew. I make a very hearty chicken stew, doctor, the way my mother taught me, with the thick broth and the little pearl onions. \n\nSo I'll dish out a normal plate, just a regular portion for a lady my age, and I'll sit down at the kitchen table. But then... well, I finish that plate, and that heavy quiet I was telling you about is still just sitting there in the room with me. So I'll get up and go back to the stove for a little more. And then I just... I just keep going back. I'll stand right there at the counter with the serving spoon, getting another scoop, and then another. \n\nAnd by the time I finally stop and look up at the clock above the sink... maybe only an hour has passed. An hour, or maybe an hour and a half at most. And I'll realize I've stood there and eaten enough for two or three entire meals, all in that short little window of time before the evening news even starts. It's just an awful lot of food to put away so fast, and I don't even really taste it after that first bowl. It just disappears so quickly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel self-critical or guilty after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, the guilt... it's heavy. It's so terribly heavy. I think \"ashamed\" is really the best word for it. Afterward, when the pot is empty and I put the spoon down, and that awful quiet rushes right back into the kitchen... I just feel so deeply ashamed and sad about what I've done. \n\nBecause, you see, cooking and food... they used to mean something entirely different to me. When my husband was alive, and when the children were still little and running around the house, food was all about family. It was about caring for the people I loved. I would spend all Sunday afternoon in that kitchen, roasting a chicken or making a big pot of my mother's gravy, and the whole house would smell so warm and inviting. We'd all sit around the table together, and it was a blessing, you know? It was how I showed my family that I loved them, how I kept them strong and healthy. \n\nBut now... now I look at what I'm doing, standing there alone at the counter, just shoveling it in without even tasting it, and I realize I've taken this beautiful thing that used to mean family and caring, and I've turned it into this... this ugly little secret. \n\nAnd that's exactly what it is, a secret that I feel like I have to hide from everyone. Especially the people at church. I've been going to that same parish for forty years, doctor. I'm on the hospitality committee, I bake the casseroles for the potlucks and the funeral luncheons, I organize the bake sales. The ladies there, they all know me as someone who cooks to serve the Lord and serve the community. If Martha or Susan or any of those ladies knew what I was doing in my own kitchen on a Tuesday afternoon... if they knew I was just gorging myself on food meant for three people... oh, I think I would just die of embarrassment. I really would. \n\nSo I wash the pot as fast as I can, and I scrub the bowl, and I put everything away so there's no evidence left. And then I just sit there at the clean table, feeling so ashamed of myself, sometimes just crying into my hands, wondering how I let something so good turn into something I have to hide from the world. It just makes the sadness so much worse, doctor. It really does.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you are eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Enjoy it? The taste, you mean? Well... you would certainly think I must be enjoying it, wouldn't you, to eat so much of it at one time. But... no. No, I don't think I do. And that's really the strangest part of all this, doctor. \n\nYou see, my whole life, I have always been such a slow eater. My mother, God rest her soul, she was very strict about table manners when my sisters and I were growing up. She used to make us put our forks down on the rim of the plate between every single bite, and we had to chew our food twenty times before swallowing. She'd sit right there at the head of the table and watch us to make sure we weren't rushing. So because of that, I've always been the last one to finish my meal. Even when my husband was alive—he was a fast eater, he'd be completely done with his roast beef and potatoes, and I'd still be cutting up my first slice of meat, just taking my time and savoring it. \n\nBut lately... when this happens, when that awful quiet in the house pushes me into the kitchen... it's like I'm a completely different person. I eat so incredibly fast. I don't even sit down at the table like a civilized person, I just stand right there by the counter. I'll be leaning against the edge of the counter, and I just rush through it. I am hardly even tasting the food at all. It's like my hand has a mind of its own, bringing the spoon up, and before I can even really register the flavor of the broth or the texture of the beans, the next bite is already in my mouth. Just one right after the other, as fast as I can manage. \n\nSo, no, I can't rightly say I'm enjoying the taste. I'm not really tasting it at all, to be perfectly honest with you. It's just going down so fast, and it feels so frantic. Which is just... well, it's just so unlike me, doctor. I've always taken such pride in savoring a good, home-cooked meal, and now I'm just standing there shoveling it in like someone is going to snatch the bowl away from me if I don't finish it that very second. It's a terrible shame, really, to waste good food that way without even enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, that's exactly it. That is the whole reason I finally decided I needed to come in and talk to somebody about this. Because... well, it's just so frightening when it happens. \n\nI'll be standing there, like I was telling you, right by the stove with the serving spoon in my hand, eating those warm comfort foods that just remind me of better days. And in my head, I am completely aware of what I'm doing. I will literally say to myself, sometimes even out loud to the empty room, I'll say, \"Maria, that is enough. You are full, put the spoon down.\" I scold myself just like my mother used to scold me when I'd try to sneak a second piece of bread before supper. \n\nBut... it's the strangest thing. Even while I'm telling myself to stop, my hand just... it still goes right back to the pot. It's like my arm doesn't even belong to me anymore. I just can't make myself stop. No matter how much I tell myself to quit, the hand just dips back into the stew or the beans and brings up another spoonful. It's a terrible feeling, doctor, to be standing in your own kitchen, in the house you've lived in for fifty years, and feel like you have absolutely no say over what your own body is doing. It's exactly what brings me in today, because once I start eating, I just cannot make myself stop, no matter how hard I try to talk some sense into myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And then the next thing I know, I'm standing at the stove making that macaroni. \n\nSo I don't know if the sadness directly fires it off like a starting pistol, or if I'm just wandering around my empty house trying to find something, anything at all, to do with my hands when the grief gets too loud. I just know my heart is broken, doctor, and the eating is what happens when I can't bear the quiet anymore. I'm sorry if that doesn't exactly answer your question, I just don't know the proper medical way to explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Within an hour or two... well, yes, I suppose it all happens rather quickly once the ball gets rolling. \n\nYou see, some afternoons... usually after I get home from choir practice over at the church, or maybe after I've been out working in the garden. The weeds have been just terrible this year, especially around my tomatoes, and my knees aren't what they used to be, so I get quite tired out by the time I come back indoors. But I'll come inside, take off my shoes, wash my hands, and I'll go to the stove to fix myself a plate of something warm. Usually some rice and beans, or maybe a nice chicken stew. I make a very hearty chicken stew, doctor, the way my mother taught me, with the thick broth and the little pearl onions. \n\nSo I'll dish out a normal plate, just a regular portion for a lady my age, and I'll sit down at the kitchen table. But then... well, I finish that plate, and that heavy quiet I was telling you about is still just sitting there in the room with me. So I'll get up and go back to the stove for a little more. And then I just... I just keep going back. I'll stand right there at the counter with the serving spoon, getting another scoop, and then another. \n\nAnd by the time I finally stop and look up at the clock above the sink... maybe only an hour has passed. An hour, or maybe an hour and a half at most. And I'll realize I've stood there and eaten enough for two or three entire meals, all in that short little window of time before the evening news even starts. It's just an awful lot of food to put away so fast, and I don't even really taste it after that first bowl. It just disappears so quickly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel self-critical or guilty after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, the guilt... it's heavy. It's so terribly heavy. I think \"ashamed\" is really the best word for it. Afterward, when the pot is empty and I put the spoon down, and that awful quiet rushes right back into the kitchen... I just feel so deeply ashamed and sad about what I've done. \n\nBecause, you see, cooking and food... they used to mean something entirely different to me. When my husband was alive, and when the children were still little and running around the house, food was all about family. It was about caring for the people I loved. I would spend all Sunday afternoon in that kitchen, roasting a chicken or making a big pot of my mother's gravy, and the whole house would smell so warm and inviting. We'd all sit around the table together, and it was a blessing, you know? It was how I showed my family that I loved them, how I kept them strong and healthy. \n\nBut now... now I look at what I'm doing, standing there alone at the counter, just shoveling it in without even tasting it, and I realize I've taken this beautiful thing that used to mean family and caring, and I've turned it into this... this ugly little secret. \n\nAnd that's exactly what it is, a secret that I feel like I have to hide from everyone. Especially the people at church. I've been going to that same parish for forty years, doctor. I'm on the hospitality committee, I bake the casseroles for the potlucks and the funeral luncheons, I organize the bake sales. The ladies there, they all know me as someone who cooks to serve the Lord and serve the community. If Martha or Susan or any of those ladies knew what I was doing in my own kitchen on a Tuesday afternoon... if they knew I was just gorging myself on food meant for three people... oh, I think I would just die of embarrassment. I really would. \n\nSo I wash the pot as fast as I can, and I scrub the bowl, and I put everything away so there's no evidence left. And then I just sit there at the clean table, feeling so ashamed of myself, sometimes just crying into my hands, wondering how I let something so good turn into something I have to hide from the world. It just makes the sadness so much worse, doctor. It really does.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you are eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Enjoy it? The taste, you mean? Well... you would certainly think I must be enjoying it, wouldn't you, to eat so much of it at one time. But... no. No, I don't think I do. And that's really the strangest part of all this, doctor. \n\nYou see, my whole life, I have always been such a slow eater. My mother, God rest her soul, she was very strict about table manners when my sisters and I were growing up. She used to make us put our forks down on the rim of the plate between every single bite, and we had to chew our food twenty times before swallowing. She'd sit right there at the head of the table and watch us to make sure we weren't rushing. So because of that, I've always been the last one to finish my meal. Even when my husband was alive—he was a fast eater, he'd be completely done with his roast beef and potatoes, and I'd still be cutting up my first slice of meat, just taking my time and savoring it. \n\nBut lately... when this happens, when that awful quiet in the house pushes me into the kitchen... it's like I'm a completely different person. I eat so incredibly fast. I don't even sit down at the table like a civilized person, I just stand right there by the counter. I'll be leaning against the edge of the counter, and I just rush through it. I am hardly even tasting the food at all. It's like my hand has a mind of its own, bringing the spoon up, and before I can even really register the flavor of the broth or the texture of the beans, the next bite is already in my mouth. Just one right after the other, as fast as I can manage. \n\nSo, no, I can't rightly say I'm enjoying the taste. I'm not really tasting it at all, to be perfectly honest with you. It's just going down so fast, and it feels so frantic. Which is just... well, it's just so unlike me, doctor. I've always taken such pride in savoring a good, home-cooked meal, and now I'm just standing there shoveling it in like someone is going to snatch the bowl away from me if I don't finish it that very second. It's a terrible shame, really, to waste good food that way without even enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, that's exactly it. That is the whole reason I finally decided I needed to come in and talk to somebody about this. Because... well, it's just so frightening when it happens. \n\nI'll be standing there, like I was telling you, right by the stove with the serving spoon in my hand, eating those warm comfort foods that just remind me of better days. And in my head, I am completely aware of what I'm doing. I will literally say to myself, sometimes even out loud to the empty room, I'll say, \"Maria, that is enough. You are full, put the spoon down.\" I scold myself just like my mother used to scold me when I'd try to sneak a second piece of bread before supper. \n\nBut... it's the strangest thing. Even while I'm telling myself to stop, my hand just... it still goes right back to the pot. It's like my arm doesn't even belong to me anymore. I just can't make myself stop. No matter how much I tell myself to quit, the hand just dips back into the stew or the beans and brings up another spoonful. It's a terrible feeling, doctor, to be standing in your own kitchen, in the house you've lived in for fifty years, and feel like you have absolutely no say over what your own body is doing. It's exactly what brings me in today, because once I start eating, I just cannot make myself stop, no matter how hard I try to talk some sense into myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, it's exactly like I was just saying a moment ago, doctor. That complete lack of say over the amount... it's really the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair today. What brings me in is that once I start eating those comfort foods, the amounts just get completely away from me. 

I'll make a big pot of something, maybe a heavy stew or macaroni, the kind of recipe that is meant to feed a whole family. And I'll be standing there, watching the level of the food go down, down, down in the pot. I can see exactly how much I'm taking. And I will literally tell myself, "Maria, that is enough. Look at how much you've already had, you're going to make yourself sick." I try so hard to control it, I really do. I try to put a limit on it. 

But even while I'm looking at the empty space in the pan where four servings used to be, my hand still goes right back to the pot. It's like I can't make myself stop, no matter how much I know I've crossed the line of a normal portion. I've been cooking and portioning out meals since I was fourteen years old, back when I had to leave high school to help my mother keep house, so I know what a proper amount of food looks like. I know it. But in those moments, all that knowledge just flies right out the window. I just keep scooping and scooping, completely unable to rein in how much I'm putting into my mouth. It's a dreadful feeling, just dreadful, to watch yourself eat that much and not be able to put the brakes on.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit hard to explain, really. You see, I'm home most of the day now, have been for years and years, and the house gets so quiet. The days can feel awfully long when it's just you. So I cook. I make these big pots of things, you know, macaroni, thick stews, potatoes—real comfort foods that remind me of when the house was full of people and noise. \n\nBut what brings me in today is... well, it's what happens after I make them. Once I start eating those comfort foods, something just comes over me. I'll have a bowl, and I'll sit in my armchair to watch my programs, and I know I'm done. I tell myself, I even say it right out loud sometimes, I say, \"Maria, that is enough.\" I know I don't need another bite. But then... it's like my brain and my body aren't talking to each other anymore. \n\nBefore I even really decide to get up, my hand just goes right back to the pot on the stove. I'll scoop out more, and I just keep eating, like I can’t make myself stop. I'll be sitting there, telling myself the whole time that I shouldn't be doing this, that I've had plenty, but my hand just keeps going back for another spoonful, and then another. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing, because I've always been a sensible woman, but with this... I just can't seem to make myself put the spoon down.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you every time you cook and eat these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not every single time I cook, thank goodness. If it were every time, I don't know what I'd do with myself. But... well, it's been happening a lot more lately. You see, my dear friend Betty passed away a few months ago. We used to do everything together. We'd go to choir practice on Tuesdays, and then we had our little community garden plot we'd tend to on Thursdays. We'd grow the most wonderful tomatoes, doctor, big beefsteak ones. \n\nSince she's been gone, I still go to choir, and I still go down to the garden, but coming home... coming home is the hardest part. The house is just so empty. So I'll come back from singing, or from pulling weeds, and it's usually those quiet afternoons, maybe once or twice a week, that it really hits me. I'll make a pot of something warm, thinking it'll make me feel better, make the house smell like it used to. And that's when it happens. Usually once or twice a week, just sitting there in the quiet after I've been out doing the things Betty and I used to do together. It's like the silence is so loud I just have to fill it up, and before I know it, I'm back at the stove, scooping out another bowl, even though I'm not hungry in the slightest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor... guilty doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... well, it's this awful, heavy feeling that just settles right in my chest. You see, cooking used to be such a joyous thing for me. When my husband was alive, God rest his soul, and the kids were still running around the house, making a big pot of stew or a casserole... it was how I showed them I loved them. It meant family. It meant caring for the people I cherished most. I'd stand at that stove for hours, chopping and stirring, and it was a labor of love, it really was. \n\nBut now... now when I do this, when I eat and eat until I'm just stuffed and miserable... afterward, I just sit there in my chair, looking at the empty bowls, and I feel so deeply ashamed. It's like a dark cloud just comes right down over me. I feel so sad, doctor, just incredibly sad. Because I look at what I've done, and I realize I've taken something beautiful—something that used to bring people together around my table—and I've turned it into this... this secret, shameful thing. \n\nI'll be washing the dishes afterward, scrubbing the pot, and I just cry sometimes. I think to myself, \"What if the ladies at church knew?\" What if Pastor Thomas or Mary from the altar guild found out that I sit alone in my house and just gorge myself on macaroni until I can barely breathe? I'd die of embarrassment, I really would. I have to hide it from everyone. I make sure to take the trash out right away so no one sees the empty food containers, and if someone drops by, I rush to put everything away. It's exhausting, carrying around this secret, and the shame of it... it just eats away at me, it really does. It's a terrible thing to feel like you have to hide yourself from the people you pray with on Sundays.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, that's just it, isn't it? That's exactly the crux of the whole terrible matter. When you ask if I'm in control... oh, heavens, no. Not at all. And that's what's so frightening about it, really. \n\nLike I was trying to explain earlier, when I sit down with that first bowl of macaroni or stew—and I make a very good stew, doctor, with tender carrots and those little pearl onions, just the way my mother taught me—I'll be sitting there in my armchair, the one by the window where the light is best in the afternoon. And I'll finish that bowl, and I'll say it right out loud, just as clear as day. I'll say, \"Maria, that is enough. You are full, you are satisfied, and you do not need another bite.\" \n\nI know it in my head. I feel it in my stomach. But then... it's like a switch flips, or like someone else is driving the car, if you know what I mean. My hand just picks up that spoon, or the bowl, and before I can even process what's happening, I'm walking right back into the kitchen. I'll stand over the stove, and I'll just scoop more out. And the whole time—this is the part that makes me feel like I'm losing my mind—the whole time I'm doing it, I'm telling myself to stop. I'm thinking, \"Why are you doing this? Put the spoon down, Maria.\" But my hand just keeps moving. It goes from the pot to my mouth, over and over, like I'm a machine or something. I can't make myself stop, no matter how hard I try or how much I scold myself. It's like my own body just isn't listening to me anymore. I'm just along for the ride, and it's a dreadful, dreadful feeling to have no say in what your own two hands are doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these episodes of eating without control for more than 3 months?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that for a moment, doctor. Let me just look at the calendar in my head. You see, Betty passed away right at the tail end of the summer. I remember it so clearly because our community garden was just starting to turn, you know? It's that time of year when the nights get a little bit cooler and the tomatoes get those little splits in their skins. We had just picked the last of the really good, big beefsteak ones before she went into the hospital. And then the funeral... oh, it was a lovely service, it really was. Pastor Thomas spoke so beautifully about her generous spirit, and the choir sang her favorite hymns. \n\nBut anyway, after the funeral, for the first week or two, the ladies from the church were bringing by so many casseroles and baked zitis and things that I didn't even have to turn on my oven. It was only after all that stopped, when folks went back to their normal lives and I was left all alone in that quiet house, that I had to start cooking for myself again. And that is exactly when this whole dreadful business began. So, if I count it back... yes, it has been right about three months now. \n\nIt's been going on for about three months, though I tell you, some days it feels like it's been a lifetime. Every week just stretches out so long without her. I keep hoping, you know, I keep thinking to myself that maybe when spring comes around and I need to get the soil ready for planting again, maybe I'll finally snap out of this terrible habit. But yes, for these past three months, ever since my dear Betty left this earth, it's been exactly like I told you. Three solid months of sitting in that chair, getting up, eating when I know perfectly well I shouldn't, and just feeling so terribly ashamed of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen to you regularly, like on a weekly basis?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, they do. It's... it's almost every week, to be perfectly honest with you. Ever since my dear friend Betty passed, like I was telling you, this whole awful thing has been happening most weeks. Usually, I'd say it's about once or twice a week. \n\nAnd it's always, always in those terribly quiet afternoons. You see, I'll come home from choir practice—I still sing alto, have for near forty years now, though my voice isn't quite as strong as it was when my husband was alive—or I'll come inside from working out in the dirt, doing my gardening. I still try to keep up with the weeding, even though my knees just ache terribly if I kneel for too long. But I'll come inside, wash the soil off my hands at the kitchen sink, and the house is just... oh, doctor, it's just so still. It's a heavy, lonely kind of quiet. \n\nBetty and I used to sit out on the porch with a glass of iced tea after we pulled the weeds, just talking about the Sunday sermon or how the hydrangeas were doing. Now, there's just nobody. Just me and the ticking clock in the hallway. And that's exactly when it gets me. Once or twice a week, in those long, empty afternoons after choir or gardening, I'll start making a pot of macaroni or stew just to have something to do, to make the house smell like a home again, I suppose. And then before I know it, I'm eating and eating just to fill up all that quiet. So yes, it's a regular weekly thing now, Heaven help me, and I just don't know how to break out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're eating like this and can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Anxious... well, let me just ponder on that a second, doctor. Stressed. You know, I don't think I would use those particular words. \n\nWhen you say \"stressed,\" I think about back when my husband was alive and he got laid off from the mill back in the seventies. Now that was stress. I'd be up all night, pacing the floorboards, worrying over how we were going to afford the children's school shoes or keep the heat on during those terrible blizzards we used to get. My heart would just hammer in my chest, and I'd be running around all day trying to make a dollar stretch into five. \n\nBut this... what happens now with the eating... it isn't like that at all. It's almost the exact opposite, if that makes any sense. When I'm standing there at the stove, scooping out that macaroni or the stew, I'm not jittery or worked up. It's more like... oh, how do I explain it? It's like a thick fog just rolls right into my kitchen and wraps around my head. I don't feel nervous. I just feel sort of blank, or numb, just trying to fill up that awful, heavy silence I was telling you about. It's just me and the spoon and the quiet. \n\nNow, afterward... well, afterward I feel just sick with myself, and terribly ashamed, like I said before. I'll look at the empty pot and think, \"Maria, what on earth have you done?\" I'll just want to cry looking at the mess. But during the actual eating? No, I wouldn't call it anxiety or stress. I'm not panicked while my hand is moving. It's just a deep, deep loneliness, I suppose, and a sort of going through the motions to keep the quiet away. But no, not stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens because you're trying to cope with your feelings of loneliness and sadness after Betty passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you put it that way, doctor... I suppose... yes. Yes, I think you might have hit the nail right on the head there. \n\nYou know, it's funny how you can be doing something, just going through the motions day after day, and not really see the forest for the trees until someone else points it out to you. But when you say it out loud like that... coping with the loneliness... it makes a terrible amount of sense. \n\nBecause before Betty got sick, I never did this. Never in all my seventy-eight years. My husband, God rest his soul, he used to say I ate like a bird. \"Maria,\" he'd say, \"you're going to blow away in a stiff breeze if you don't eat more of that roast.\" I always just had a small plate, always stopped when I was full. Even when he passed, twenty years ago now, I didn't turn to the pantry. I turned to my church, and to my garden, and to my friends. To Betty. \n\nShe was the one who came over and sat with me every single afternoon that first year after he died. We'd just sit and shell peas on the porch, or fold laundry, not even talking sometimes, just... being there. Having another breathing soul in the house. \n\nAnd now she's gone, too. And my children, well, they're all grown and moved away—my eldest is out in California, and my youngest is up in Chicago, they have their own lives and their own families, and I can't expect them to be calling their old mother every day. So it's just me. And when I come into this house, and it's so quiet you can hear the refrigerator humming from two rooms away... I just feel this great big hole right in the center of my chest. It physically aches, doctor. It really does. \n\nAnd I think... I think when I start cooking, and then when I start eating all that macaroni or that stew... for just those few minutes, my mouth is busy, and my stomach is getting full, and it distracts me from how empty the rest of the house is. It's like I'm trying to fill up that hole in my chest with food, because I don't have Betty to fill it up with conversation anymore. So yes. Yes, I think you're exactly right. It's the sadness. It's just... it's just being so terribly, terribly sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the sadness you feel after Betty passed away directly triggers these episodes of eating without control?\n\nPatient: Directly triggers... well, now, I just don't know if I can say for sure how that part works, doctor. That word, 'trigger'... it sounds so mechanical, doesn't it? Like flipping a switch on the wall to turn on the porch light. You flip it up, the light comes on. But my mind, well, at my age, I don't think it works quite like a simple light switch. \n\nI mean, I know I just said a moment ago that the sadness is why I'm doing it. And I do believe that's the truth of it, I really do. But as for it being a... a direct trigger? I couldn't rightly say if it's that straightforward. Because sometimes the sadness is just there all day long, sitting on my chest heavy as a wet wool blanket, from the minute I wake up and see that the sun is shining and realize Betty isn't here to enjoy it. I'll look out the kitchen window at the hydrangeas—they're blooming so beautifully this year, big blue heads on them, she would have just loved them—and I'll feel that terrible ache. But I don't go eating right then in the morning. \n\nIt's only later. So is the sadness triggering it, or is it the quiet? Or is it both of them getting together in the afternoon and just wearing me down to the bone? I just... I couldn't tell you the exact science of it. I'll just be looking at Betty's old gardening gloves—I kept them, you know, they're still sitting on the porch rail right where she left them the Tuesday before she went into the hospital—and I'll feel so completely lost. And then the next thing I know, I'm standing at the stove making that macaroni. \n\nSo I don't know if the sadness directly fires it off like a starting pistol, or if I'm just wandering around my empty house trying to find something, anything at all, to do with my hands when the grief gets too loud. I just know my heart is broken, doctor, and the eating is what happens when I can't bear the quiet anymore. I'm sorry if that doesn't exactly answer your question, I just don't know the proper medical way to explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Within an hour or two... well, yes, I suppose it all happens rather quickly once the ball gets rolling. \n\nYou see, some afternoons... usually after I get home from choir practice over at the church, or maybe after I've been out working in the garden. The weeds have been just terrible this year, especially around my tomatoes, and my knees aren't what they used to be, so I get quite tired out by the time I come back indoors. But I'll come inside, take off my shoes, wash my hands, and I'll go to the stove to fix myself a plate of something warm. Usually some rice and beans, or maybe a nice chicken stew. I make a very hearty chicken stew, doctor, the way my mother taught me, with the thick broth and the little pearl onions. \n\nSo I'll dish out a normal plate, just a regular portion for a lady my age, and I'll sit down at the kitchen table. But then... well, I finish that plate, and that heavy quiet I was telling you about is still just sitting there in the room with me. So I'll get up and go back to the stove for a little more. And then I just... I just keep going back. I'll stand right there at the counter with the serving spoon, getting another scoop, and then another. \n\nAnd by the time I finally stop and look up at the clock above the sink... maybe only an hour has passed. An hour, or maybe an hour and a half at most. And I'll realize I've stood there and eaten enough for two or three entire meals, all in that short little window of time before the evening news even starts. It's just an awful lot of food to put away so fast, and I don't even really taste it after that first bowl. It just disappears so quickly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel self-critical or guilty after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, the guilt... it's heavy. It's so terribly heavy. I think \"ashamed\" is really the best word for it. Afterward, when the pot is empty and I put the spoon down, and that awful quiet rushes right back into the kitchen... I just feel so deeply ashamed and sad about what I've done. \n\nBecause, you see, cooking and food... they used to mean something entirely different to me. When my husband was alive, and when the children were still little and running around the house, food was all about family. It was about caring for the people I loved. I would spend all Sunday afternoon in that kitchen, roasting a chicken or making a big pot of my mother's gravy, and the whole house would smell so warm and inviting. We'd all sit around the table together, and it was a blessing, you know? It was how I showed my family that I loved them, how I kept them strong and healthy. \n\nBut now... now I look at what I'm doing, standing there alone at the counter, just shoveling it in without even tasting it, and I realize I've taken this beautiful thing that used to mean family and caring, and I've turned it into this... this ugly little secret. \n\nAnd that's exactly what it is, a secret that I feel like I have to hide from everyone. Especially the people at church. I've been going to that same parish for forty years, doctor. I'm on the hospitality committee, I bake the casseroles for the potlucks and the funeral luncheons, I organize the bake sales. The ladies there, they all know me as someone who cooks to serve the Lord and serve the community. If Martha or Susan or any of those ladies knew what I was doing in my own kitchen on a Tuesday afternoon... if they knew I was just gorging myself on food meant for three people... oh, I think I would just die of embarrassment. I really would. \n\nSo I wash the pot as fast as I can, and I scrub the bowl, and I put everything away so there's no evidence left. And then I just sit there at the clean table, feeling so ashamed of myself, sometimes just crying into my hands, wondering how I let something so good turn into something I have to hide from the world. It just makes the sadness so much worse, doctor. It really does.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you are eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Enjoy it? The taste, you mean? Well... you would certainly think I must be enjoying it, wouldn't you, to eat so much of it at one time. But... no. No, I don't think I do. And that's really the strangest part of all this, doctor. \n\nYou see, my whole life, I have always been such a slow eater. My mother, God rest her soul, she was very strict about table manners when my sisters and I were growing up. She used to make us put our forks down on the rim of the plate between every single bite, and we had to chew our food twenty times before swallowing. She'd sit right there at the head of the table and watch us to make sure we weren't rushing. So because of that, I've always been the last one to finish my meal. Even when my husband was alive—he was a fast eater, he'd be completely done with his roast beef and potatoes, and I'd still be cutting up my first slice of meat, just taking my time and savoring it. \n\nBut lately... when this happens, when that awful quiet in the house pushes me into the kitchen... it's like I'm a completely different person. I eat so incredibly fast. I don't even sit down at the table like a civilized person, I just stand right there by the counter. I'll be leaning against the edge of the counter, and I just rush through it. I am hardly even tasting the food at all. It's like my hand has a mind of its own, bringing the spoon up, and before I can even really register the flavor of the broth or the texture of the beans, the next bite is already in my mouth. Just one right after the other, as fast as I can manage. \n\nSo, no, I can't rightly say I'm enjoying the taste. I'm not really tasting it at all, to be perfectly honest with you. It's just going down so fast, and it feels so frantic. Which is just... well, it's just so unlike me, doctor. I've always taken such pride in savoring a good, home-cooked meal, and now I'm just standing there shoveling it in like someone is going to snatch the bowl away from me if I don't finish it that very second. It's a terrible shame, really, to waste good food that way without even enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, that's exactly it. That is the whole reason I finally decided I needed to come in and talk to somebody about this. Because... well, it's just so frightening when it happens. \n\nI'll be standing there, like I was telling you, right by the stove with the serving spoon in my hand, eating those warm comfort foods that just remind me of better days. And in my head, I am completely aware of what I'm doing. I will literally say to myself, sometimes even out loud to the empty room, I'll say, \"Maria, that is enough. You are full, put the spoon down.\" I scold myself just like my mother used to scold me when I'd try to sneak a second piece of bread before supper. \n\nBut... it's the strangest thing. Even while I'm telling myself to stop, my hand just... it still goes right back to the pot. It's like my arm doesn't even belong to me anymore. I just can't make myself stop. No matter how much I tell myself to quit, the hand just dips back into the stew or the beans and brings up another spoonful. It's a terrible feeling, doctor, to be standing in your own kitchen, in the house you've lived in for fifty years, and feel like you have absolutely no say over what your own body is doing. It's exactly what brings me in today, because once I start eating, I just cannot make myself stop, no matter how hard I try to talk some sense into myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's exactly like I was just saying a moment ago, doctor. That complete lack of say over the amount... it's really the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair today. What brings me in is that once I start eating those comfort foods, the amounts just get completely away from me. \n\nI'll make a big pot of something, maybe a heavy stew or macaroni, the kind of recipe that is meant to feed a whole family. And I'll be standing there, watching the level of the food go down, down, down in the pot. I can see exactly how much I'm taking. And I will literally tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough. Look at how much you've already had, you're going to make yourself sick.\" I try so hard to control it, I really do. I try to put a limit on it. \n\nBut even while I'm looking at the empty space in the pan where four servings used to be, my hand still goes right back to the pot. It's like I can't make myself stop, no matter how much I know I've crossed the line of a normal portion. I've been cooking and portioning out meals since I was fourteen years old, back when I had to leave high school to help my mother keep house, so I know what a proper amount of food looks like. I know it. But in those moments, all that knowledge just flies right out the window. I just keep scooping and scooping, completely unable to rein in how much I'm putting into my mouth. It's a dreadful feeling, just dreadful, to watch yourself eat that much and not be able to put the brakes on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
